Τὴν ἀπρόοπτον δέξεται τιμωρίαν. Οὕτω παραινῶν καὶ παρ᾿ αὐτῶν εἰς μάχην Πρωτοποιεῖ τὴν σφαγὴν τοῖς ἀξίοις. 130 Ὁ δὲ στρατηγὸς ὁ κρατῶν Νικηφόρος Οὐκ εἶχε καιρὸν ἀγνοεῖν τὰς τοῦ πλάνου Ἅσπερ παρέλκων ἔπλεκε στρουφουργίας. Ἦν γάρ τις αὐτὰς τὰς ὁδοὺς πραμηνύων (8), Ἐκ τῶν ἀκάσμως ἔνδον ἐγκεκλεισμένων. 135 Οὕτω στρατάρχας, ἄνδρας εὐθεῖς ἱππότας, Φαλαγγοσώρας, πεζοτοξοχράτας Εἰς ἓν συνῆψε τοῖς στρατηγέταις ἅμα, Μέσον δὲ τούτων, εὐτελὴς ὡς ἱππότης, Σταθεὶς (9) ἀφῆκεν ὁπλικωτάτους λόγους· 140 Ῥώμης τὰ νεῦρα, τέκνα, σύνδουλοι, φίλοι, Ἰδοὺ καθ᾿ ἡμῶν καὶ πάλιν τὸ θηρίον Κενὰς (10) ἔσωθεν ἤρξατο σκευωρίας· Ἰδοὺ τὸ δεινὸν τῆς Χαρύβδεως στόμα
filiis mori, atque prophetæ verba contempserit, et nobis occidetur, et involutus tenebris non opinatum accipiet supplicium. Sic milites hortatus, eos ad pugnam præivit, iter sternens ad cædem meritam. Dux autem supremus Nicephorus opportune noscebat deceptoris fraudes, quas incassum struebat : erat enim, qui ipsas nuntiabat insidias ex iis, qui furtim intus clausi erant. Quare militum duces, excelsos equites, legionarios, pedites arcitenentes in unum collegit cum præfectia simul. Interque hos medius, tanquam privatus eques stans


[1025] {#pg.113.1025}
᾿Αναθορῆσαι μαίνεται Ῥώμης κράτος· 145 Ἰδοὺ (11) συριγμοὺς ὁ δράκων χεῖας μέσον Κήρυκας ὀργῆς καὶ σφαγῆς ἀναπνέει, Φυσᾷ τὸν ἰὸν καὶ βιάζει τὴν φύσιν. ᾿Αλλ᾿ ἔστιν αὐτοῦ τὸ θράσος δειλανδρία. Μηδεὶς φοβηθεὶς ὀπίσθω φευγέτω· 150 θυμὸς γὰρ αὐτῷ μέχρι τῶν κτύπων μόνων. Ἴωμεν ἀνθ᾿ ὧν εὐσθενῶς ὡπλισμένοι· Ἔχουσι σάρκας καὶ τὰ τῆς ῎Αγαρ τέκνα (12). Οὐκ ἔστιν αὐτοῖς σὰρξ σίδηρος, ἢ λίθος. ᾿Αλγοῦσιν, ἂν λάβωσι, Ῥωμαίων πλέον· 155 ᾿Αργοῦσιν, αὐτῶν οἱ κοπῶσιν αἱ χεῖρες· Πίπτουσιν εἰς γῆν, οἱ κοπῶσιν οἱ πόδες. Μηδεὶς νοσήσει τὴν πικρὰν ἀνανδρίαν· Μηδεὶς φοβηθεὶς οὐ φανεῖ Ῥώμης κλάδος. Μηδεὶς νόθον γέννημα καὶ σπορὰν ξένην,
hæc emisit verba ad bellum excitantia : « Romæ robur, filii, conservi, amici, ecce adversum nos iterum bellua molita est intus irritas insidias ; ecce Charybdis os terribile Romanum absorbere imperium nititur : ecce sibila Draco lustro in medio emittit, iræ atque cædis nuntia, spirat venenum, et vim infert naturæ. Attamen timida est ejus audacia : nemo exteritus vertat terga fugæ, namque illi est animus summis tantum labiis. Armati itaque eamus contra illos stuepue, carnei sunt etiam Agar filii, nec illis inest caro ferrea, aut saxea. Si vulnerentur, plus quam Romani, dolent ; torpescunt, si incidantur corum manus : humi procumbunt, si amputentur pedes. Nemo laboret improba desidia, nemo tre-


[1026] {#pg.113.1026}
160 Δείξειν ἑαυτὸν ἐκ κακῆς σπουδαζέτω. Αἰδώς, ἀδελφοί, τῆς φυγῆς ἡ ζημία (13). Αἰδεῖσθε φεύγειν καὶ πτοεῖσθαι τὰ ξίφη. Ποῦ γὰρ φυγοῦσιν εὑρεθῇ σωτηρία, Μένουσιν ἔνδον Κρητικῶν ὁρισμάτων; 165 Οὐκ ἔστιν ἰσχὺς τῆς ῎Αγαρ τοῖς ἐγγόνοις· Θεὸς γὰρ (14) ἡμῖν συμμαχεῖ καὶ συντρέχει. Οὕτω στομώσας τοῦ στρατοῦ τὰ ξίφη, Λόγους ἐποίει, καὶ τροπὴν πεπλασμένην, Ὡς ἂν κλαπέντες ἔνδοθεν τειχισμάτων (15) 170 Ἔξωθεν θαρσήσωσιν ἐκθεθήκεναι. Ὡς οὖν καιρὸς ἤγγισε τῶν βουλευμάτων, Χώραν (16) ἐχούσης τῆς ῥοδοχρόου τρίτην, Χειὰς ἀνώρμων, ὡς ὄφεις, οἱ τὰς κάρας Ἔχοντες, ὡς ὄνειδος (17), ἐξυρημένας. 175 Τούτων ὑπῆρχε καὶ κρότος ταῖς ἀσπίσι,
mens non appareat Romæ germen, nemo spuriam progeniem et extraneum genus in adversis se salagat ostendere. Turpis est, fratres, fuga, et periculosa ; pudeat vos fugere et pavere gladios ; nam, quo fugientes salutem invenient manentes intra Cretica confinia ? nullum est robur Agar posteris. Deus enim nos tuetur atque adjuvat. » Cum sic acuisset enses sui exercitus, insidias paravit et fictam fugam, ut decepti, qui stabant intra mœnia, foras auderent egredi. Ut igitur statutum tempus advenit, horam habente rosea die tertiam, ex antris, ut serpentes, erumpebant, capita detonsa habentes, tamquam de plebis fæce. His erat etiam clypeorum strepitus, clamorque multus, et tremoris pater metus


[1027] {#pg.113.1027}
Βοή τε πολλή, καὶ τρόμου πατὴρ φόδος, Καὶ πάντα δεινά· τοῖς δὲ σοῖς στρατηγέταις Τάξις, σιωπή, σεμνότης, προθυμία. Εἷς οὖν στρατηγῶν, εὐγενοῦς ῥίζης κλάδος, 180 Ἀνὴρ κραταιὸς εἰς στροφὴν ἀκοντίου, Βραδὺς δὲ φεύγων, καὶ ταχὺς κατατρέχων, Λόγους γλυκεῖς ἔλεξεν ἐν στρατῷ μέσον· « Πρωτοστράτηγε, πῶς λιπὼν τὸν δεσπότην Οὐχ ὡς μάγειρος τῇ βαναυσίᾳ τέχνῃ 185 Κυκῶν ἔμιξας τούτων ἐν παραινέσει, Ὡς ἂν τὸ νέκταρ ὁ γλυκὺς στεφηφόρος Ταῖς σαῖς γένηται προσταγαῖς ἀρτυσία; Ἐκεῖνος ἡμᾶς (18) νουθετῶν, ἐπιτρέπων, Τὴν χεῖρα πᾶσιν ἐξαπλῶν, εὐεργέτει. 190 Μνησθῶμεν αὐτοῦ ῥημάτων μελιῤῥύτων, Καὶ τῶν τραπεζῶν, εἶτα τῶν χαρισμάτων. Μνήσθητε, καὶ θάνωμεν, ἄνδρες, εἰς δέον, Ὑπὸ γένους ἄνακτος εὐσεβεστάτου. Μόνον πλατυνθῇ (19) σκῆπτρον τῆς μοναρχίας, 195 Καὶ γῆς ἀνάξει Ῥωμανὸς πάσης μόνος. » Ἐπεὶ δὲ πλῆθυς μυριάνδρος βαρβάρων
et undique confusio tuis autem militibus ordo, silentium, gravitas, alacritas. Unus porro militem claro natus genere, vir fortis in jactu jaculi, tardus ad fugam, et ad insectandum velox, hæc dulcia emisit verba in medio exercitu : « Supreme dux, curnam, omisso despota, veluti coquus in arte culinaria, non eum admiscui in borum adhortatione, ut dulcis dominator, instar-nectaris, tuis condimentum fieret imperiis? admonens ille, et nostri curam gerens, beneficam manum extendebat omnibus. Recordemur ejus verba melliflua, et mensas atque munera. Recordemini, et moriamur, si opus fuerit, pro familia regis plissimi. Solum extendatur sceptrum imperii, cunctoque orbi unus Romanus imperet. » Postquam vero infinita multitudo barbarorum e portis irruit, tanquam coacta ab austro


[1028] {#pg.113.1028}
Ἐκ τῶν πυλῶν ὥρμησεν, ὡς νότου νέφος, Βρυχὴν (20) ἀπειλοῦν, καὶ προδεικνύον σπόρον, Οἳ σοὶ τραπέντες εἰς φυγὴν πεπλασμένην 200 Πάγην ἐτεχνάζοντο τὴν χρηκτετόνον. Πύργον δὲ μακρὸν πάντες ἐκδεδηκότες, Καὶ τῶν πυλῶν ἔξωθεν ἐξωρμηκότες Ποινὰς ὑπεῖχον πίστεως τὰς ἀξίας. Ἐκ τεττάρων γὰρ στρατικῶς ἐγκρυμμάτων(21) 205 Κλύδων ἐγερθεὶς, καὶ σφοδρῶς ἐπιδράσας, Χειμῶνα γεννᾶν ἤρξατο πρὸς τῆς μάχης· Καὶ δυσμενῶν ἦν συμφορά, κλόνος, στόνος, Λαῶν δὲ τῶν σῶν χαρμονῇ νικηφόρος. Ὡς οὖν σταθέντες ἦσαν ἐν μεταίχμιλῳ, 210 Τὴν ἔκβασιν βλέποντες ἐστενωμένην, Οἳ τῆς ἑαυτῶν αἴτιοι φονουργίας, Ἔδαλλον εὐθὺς, ἀνεβάλλοντο πλέον, Καὶ τὴν φυγὴν ἔχοντες ἠπορημένην, — Πάντῃ γὰρ αὐτοῖς ἦν ταράττουσα βλάβη — 215 Ἔστησαν εἰς ἓν τῆς ταφῆς τὸ χωρίον. Τίς ἐκ τραγῳδῶν ἐκβοήσει τοὺς φόνους, Καὶ τὴν ἑκάστου τῆς σφαγῆς τιμωρίαν;
nubes minitans tempestatem, et mala nuntians segetibus, tui se vertentes in fictam fugam laqueum parabant in perditionem Creticam. Cuncti autem, e longis exeuntes turribus, atque portis se foras erumpentes, pœnas luebant fide eorum dignas. A quatuor namque insidiis tuorum militum æstus commotus, et vehementer tervens, proellam cœpit producere in prælio; hostibusque erat clades, tumultus, suspirium, tuis autem populis triumphale gaudium. Cum ergo essent circumventi ab aciebus, sibi præclasum aspicientes exitum, et sibi esse auctores propriæ cædis, vulnerabant statim, sed vulnerabantur magis; nullumque habentes ad salutem effugium, nex enim illis imminebat undique, constiterunt in unum sepulcri locum. Quis ex tragicis cædes enarrabit, et singulorum sævum mortis genus?


[1029] {#pg.113.1029}
Ο μὲν γὰρ αὐτῶν ἐν νεφροῖς βεβλημένος, Περιτροπὰς ἔπασχεν, ὥσπερ ἠγνόει, 220 Καὶ πρὶν χορευτῶν οὐ μαθὼν περικλάτεις, Ἄχων χορευτὴς ἦν λαβὼν ἀκοντίῳ. Ἄλλος στερηθεὶς τῆς κεκαρμένης κάρας Ἔκειτο πρηνὴς στρατικῶς ἠπλωμένος, Καὶ τοὺς ἑαυτοῦ δυστυχῶς κινῶν πόδας, 225 Χύσει κολυμβῶν ἤθελε τῶν αἱμάτων. Ἄλλος δὲ πληγεὶς τὴν κατάξηρον ῥάχιν. Κύπτων ἄκων ἦν προσκυνῶν τὸν στόλον. Ἄλλος δὲ νεκρὸς, καὶ πόδας τετμημένος, Ἀελησμένος προὔκειτο τῶν ἱππασμάτων. 230 Ἄλλος δὲ τὴν ἄναλτον ὠσθεὶς γαστέρα, Χερσὶ κατεῖχε τὴν πλοκὴν τῶν ἐντέρων, Καὶ, πρὶν μαχητὴς εὐσθενὴς κεκριμένος, Μάγειρος ἦν ἄπειρος ἐντεροπράτης (22), Οὕτως μετηλλάξαντο τὴν πανοπλίαν 235 Εἰς ἀλλοτέχνους συμφορὰς οἱ τῆς Ἄγαρ· Καὶ πᾶς τελευτῶν εἶχε τὴν τιμωρίαν, Τέχνην πονηρὰν εἰς ἀναίρεσιν βίου. Εἷς οὖν ἐκείνων ἦν ἀνὴρ Κρὴς ἱππότης, Γιγαντοειδὴς, ὃς λαβὼν τὴν ἀσπίδα, 240 Καὶ τὴν μάχαιραν, τῷ στρατηγῷ τῆς μάχης Ἐναντίως ἤλαυνε, προσβαλεῖν θέλων.
Alter quidem eorum sauciatus renibus, quas ignorabat, patiebatur vertigines, ac saltans, non edoclus motus choreæ, invitus sallator flebat ictu jaculi. Alter orbatus suo detonso capite jacebat pronus stratus ab exercitu, suosque infolix movens pedes natare videbatur rivo sanguinis. Alter autem ictus in arida dorsi spina procumbens, tuum invitus adorabat exercitum. Alter mortuus, et abscissis pedibus neglectus jacebat ad conculcationem equitum. Alter, insatiabili confossus in ventre, manibus tenebat intestinorum gyros, et qui autea censebatur pugnator strenuus, coquus fiebat inexpertus viscerum. Ita commutabant arma in alias artes calamitosas Agareni, ac quisque moriens habebat pro supplicio sævam aliquam artem ad interitum. Erat porro inter illos vir Cretensis eques gigantis specie, qui, accepto clypeo et gladio, contra pugnæ ducem agebat equum, cum eo cupiens congredi. Sed non permisit Deus eum propositum suum exsequi, percutit


[1030] {#pg.113.1030}
Ἀλλ᾿ οὐκ ἄφηκεν ἡ δίκη (23) τοῦτον φλιάται· Πλήττει γὰρ αὐτὸν ὁ κρατῶν Νικηφόρος, Τῆς ὀσφύος κάτωθεν, ὀμφαλοῦ μέσον. 245 Καὶ τοῦ μὲν εἶχε σῶμα νεκρὸν ἡ κόνις (24), Τοῦ δὲ κλέος μέγιστον ἡ πλατὺς (25) στόνυξ. Οὕτω κακῶς ἔπασχον ἐγκεκλεισμένοι· Ἐκεῖθεν ἔνθεν τοῖς φονευταῖς ἐνδίκως Οἱ τῶν ἀθέσμων πράξεων ὑπηρέται. 250 Εἰ δ᾿ ἦν τις αὐτῶν εἰς φυγὴν ὡρμημένος, Ἔμπροσθεν εἶχε φραγμοὺς (26) ἔμπονον ξίφος, Καὶ τὴν στρατηγὸν πανταχοῦ Νικηφόρον Βάλλοντα, καὶ κράζοντα τοῖς στρατηγέταις· « Βάλλωμεν, ἄνδρες, τοὺς βισθόρους λύκους. 255 Ὅμηρε, ποιητῶν λογίων ὁ τῆς μάχης Πόρρω πεφυκὼς, ὡς παρὼν δὲ συγγράφων, Ὁ πολλὰ κάμνων ἐν κενοῖς, καὶ τοὺς πόνους Διεξιὼν ἅπαντας Ἑλλήνων μάτην· Σὺ δεῦρο, καὶ βόησον ὄγκῳ ῥημάτων 260 Ῥώμης στρατοῦ καύχημα, καὶ μέγαν στόνον Ἐχθρῶν παλαιῶν, δυσμενῶν, δουλοσπόρων (27). Ἐξεῖς γὰρ ἡμὴν τὴν ἀλήθειαν τύπον, Ὡς τοῖς Ἀχαιοῖς τὴν παρέκβασιν τύπον. Οὐκ ἦν γὰρ ἔνθα τις φυγὼν ἐκ τῆς μάχης, 265 Οὐδ᾿ εἰ πτερωτὴν εἶχε βάρβαρος φύσιν.
enim illum imperans Nicephorus infra renes unbilico in medio : et hic quidem occubult stratus in pulvere, illius autem laudem maximam assecutus est gladius. Sic mala patiebantur angustiati merito hinc inde ab interfectoribus iniquarum actionum ministri. Si quis autem ex illis erat qui tentaret fugam, letalem ensem habebat sibi obicem, et imperantem ubique Nicephorum ferientem, et voce adhortantem milites, « Feriamus, viri, hos voraces lupos. » Homere, qui poeticis sermonibus prælia, licet longeabsens, ut præsens tamen conscribis, qui multum fatigaris in rebus inanibus, ac labores Græcorum omnes frustra versu complecteris, huc adsis, et verborum gravitate celebra Romani exercitus gloriam, ingentesque gemitus veterum infensorum hostium ex servili semine : habebis enim in nostris veritatem pro typo, sicut in Achæis excessum pro typo. Hic enim non erat qui ex pugna fugeret barbarus, neque si alatam haberet naturam.

Tu animum præsta, linguam tu solve meam, tu edoce nos loqui confidentius, quoniam tanta est barbaris angustia. Dilata guttur data fandi copia, arctatur enim gesta tua aggrediens: labat manns, sæpius mens refugit, contrahitur et pavescit, et cupiens tuos imitantes Christum mores pingere trepide circumcursat instar leporis. Nam tu assecutus undique victorias, rempublicam juvisti, auxisti solus. Tu ingenti labore hostium agmina redegisti in loca prærupia et impervia. Exaudi, quæso, nostros fletus humiles, tuunique ovile custodi, quod salvum fecisti : fuga hostes, et da finem laboribus, quos exercitus tuus constanter subit. Hæc quidem tu, militans cum servis tuis, licet absens, agebas, ut Mores olim, qui fugavit Amalec levans in altum manus. Verum exercitus tuus in mutuis præliis visus existens extollebatur superbia. Opus ergo erat efficaci pharmaco, ut sanaretur ab imminenti morbo. Jamvero tristi nocte habente omnia, et exercitu in gaudium effuso, vel potins in superbiam, nondumque vocato properante ad præsidia, omnis locus gladiis tanquam novaculis cinctus erat, montes, valles, rupes, antra immania. E Creticis enim effusus latebris antea tremens exercitus victus septies multam incussit militibus tuis formidinem, repente irrumpens, et præter spem prodiens. nebras, thorax namque ut lux, et ensis, et galeæ commutabant colorem vesperæ. Duces autem ruentes in fugam clypeis protegentes colla dabant se præcipites, et omnis fugiens habebat pro benefactore mare ipsum atque navium alveos. Non enim valebant cum nocte et Barbaris simul pugnare, et novas cædes facere, nisi ipse (mirabile hoc et novum auditu) ardens omnibus astans eques ex occultis, ut videbatur, equitatibus hortator fuisses, et terrefaciens solus, statimque ex fuga vertisses ad prælium eos, qui ob nocturnam pugnam erant territi. Ut ergo conversi ad insperatos impetus eduxerunt omnes acuos gladios, insonuit clypeus, obstrepuit galea, emicuit nox collaborans tecum, jacebatque omnis operarius Creticus sanguine inmersus et exanimis. Οὕτω κακῶς ἔχουσιν οἱ θεαρχίας Πράξει πονηρᾷ τὸ τρισέμφῳ (62) τὸ κράτος Ἐκ τῆς ἑαυτῶν καρδίας ὠθηκότες· 150 Ὦς οὖν ὑπῆρχεν ἔνδοθεν πεφραγμένος, Τείχει πεποιθὼς (63), ὡς σθένει στρατευμάτων, Ὁ σὸς στρατηγὸς καὶ πάλιν Νικηφόρος Κριοῖς, χελώναις, σφενδόναις ἐπιτρέπει Βαλεῖν τὸ τεῖχος, τὰς ἐπάλξεων βάσεις, 155 Τοὺς εἰς τὸ τεῖχος, πάντα σὺν προθυμίᾳ (64)
intra propugnacula, ex altis enim respiciebat turribus, gentibus suis mandavit portas claudere, atque faciem manibus tundens draco fumum emisit suspiriorum ex æstuenti pectore : nam accendisti, o rex, ignem in ejus præcordiis, tanquam scintillas, tuos immittens milites. Exsultet omnis Romanorum civitas, tripudiet mare, totus lætetur orbis : fera terribilis carnes antea devorans arctata, cibis privatur communibus; et quæ non est satiata rivis sanguinis guttæ aquæ nunc tabescens sitit. Talia habent mala, qui divini imperii tripliciter emicaus dominium factis improbis procul ab eorum corde tentant pellere. Ergo cum ilę intus communitus foret mœnibus confisus, non viribus militum, tuus dux iterum Nicephorus arietibus, testudinibus, fundis jubet percuti mœnia, et pinnarum bases, et eos,

Τῶν ὀμμάτων γὰρ κρουνὸς ἔβρει δακρύων, 160 Ὀσφρήσεως πῦρ, ἦχος ἐκ τῶν ὠτίων· Λόγων δὲ κρουνοὺς ἐξέπεμπε τὸ στόμα, Καὶ χεὶρ ῥοὰς ἔβλυζε πολλῶν αἱμάτων· Ἐκ τῆς στροφῆς πάσχουσα τῶν ἀκοντίων. Οὕτως ἐκεῖνος, ὡς φανῇ σὸς οἰκέτης, 165 Ἐπλήττετο ζέοντι καρδίας πόθῳ. Πλὴν εἶχε καιρὸν ἐμποδὼν τῶν πραγμάτων Βάλλοντα τοῦτον ἀντὶ μυρίων πόνων. Ἦν οὖν βαδίζων, ἣν τρέχων ἄνω κάτω, Κλαίων, παραινῶν, καὶ παρορμῶν τὸν στόλον. 170 Ἴδειτο καὶ γὰρ τοὺς πόνους τοῦ σοῦ κράτους, Κενῶς δι᾿ αὐτοῦ μὴ περιβλέψας ὄκνος Ἐν τοῖς ἀθέσμοις Κρητικοῖς ἀναπλάσθαι. . Ὁ δὲ στρατηγὸς σφενδονοστρόφων, ἄναξ, Ποιεῖ γέλωτος ἄξιον τι καὶ πλέον. 175 Ἐν σφενδόνῃ γὰρ νωθρὸν ἐμβαλὼν ὄνον (65)
qui supra mœnia stabant, et cuneta alacriter facere, perficere, operari contra hostes. Illo loquente lusciniæ instar fatigabatur quinque sensuum numerus, nam fons lacrymarum fluebat ex oculis, naris spirabant ignem, resonabant aures, os autem verborum emittebat flumen, manusque fundebat rivos multi sanguinis in ejaculandia allaborans telis. Ita ille, ut videretur servus tuus, flagrabat ardenti cordis desiderio. Verumtamen tempus erat impedimento rebus quod eum angebat præ mille doloribus. Quare circuibat, excurrebat hac illac flous, adhortans, et confirmans exercitum. Verebatur enim labores tuorum militum, ne sensim in eos irrepens segnities Creticis iniquis pares redderet. Dux autem funditorum, o rex, facit quid risu dignum maxime. Funda enim inserens pigrum asinum jubet

projici vivum in asinos asinum. Quare hi conversi ad eum arctandum vinculis projiciunt infelicem per aeris semitas : ipse vero se agitabat, extendebat pedes, per aerem gradiebatur rusticus asinus, antea vilis in sublimi erat positus, in terra ignavus, atque tardus asinus per aerem currens terrori erat Cretensibus. Xerxes ostendit terram in aquam versam, novum antea, et omnibus terrorem incussit hostibus : tuus autem, maxime Romane, exercitus piros asinos, falcones volantes absque alis. Hunc factum dux aspiciens, sociis ducibus, subridens, tanquam a procella a curis mentem abstrahens, dixit : « Novum volucrem in cibum belluae jacentem nunc intus, o amici, accipient tanquam

O vocem inclytam, o mellifluum os ducis tui, Romane, regum decus ! quis veterum mortalium novit majus his, quæ modo sunt gesta, libros perlegens ? si quis cognovit mirum quid in Romanorum præliis volvens quæ scripta sunt ab historicis, modo, quæso, conferat cum despota victoriis illud, et apparebit leve adversus tantam sublimitatem, et improbos labores. contra nos servos tuos, o Rex, simul arma moveant, non times, dum tuis armis sit munita, civitas. Fabula valde celebrant labores Herculis, ultra veritatem exaltant illum, inclamant multum, et me terrefaciunt, ac maxima ei tribuant, forsan merito; verum amen vicit, non evertit urbes : quod si multas dixeris, sed talem quando? non fuit alter veteris Romæ rex, non fuit alter, ut inclytus despota. Ὡς δὲ κρεῶν τὸ λήχνον ἐν θηρσὶ γένος, Λύκοι, κόρον σπεύδοντες εὑρεῖν γαστέρος, Βαίνουσιν ὀργῇ καὶ κενώσει κοιλίας, 25 Πρὸ τοῦ φθάσαι χαίνοντες ἐκτάδην στόμα· Οὕτως τὸ φῦλον τῶν ἐκεῖ δουλοσπόρων Τοῖς σοῖς βιασθὲς, ποιμενάρχα, ποιμνίοις Κατεξανέστη, καὶ παρεστόμου ξίφος, Βρῶξαι προθυμοῦν σάρκας ἀντὶ (83) βρωμάτων, 30 Πίνειν (84) τε θερμὸν αἷμα Ῥωμαίων θέλον, Καὶ πρῶτα λαμπρῶς τῆς μάχης λαβεῖν κράτη. irruperunt ad cædem unam. Ut igitur in montibus educatum silvestre genus caprearum, leporum, et trepidantium damarum, dura hieme destitutarum cibo, ex altis locis conglaciatis frigore descendit gradiens per apertos campos, inquirens escas et herbosa loca : ita perversi peccati filii per loca  maris illisa fluctibus, impervia, et senta situ currebant, et valles. Ac veluti carnium avidum in er feras genus lupi, invenire festinantes cibum, gradiuntur ira, et jejunio perciti, antequam quid tollant, aperto hiantes ore : Ita horum Barbarorum servile genus a tuis oppressum, summe Pastor, gregibus contra illos insurrexit, et exacuit gladios, comedere cupiens carnes pro cibis, caldumque bibere Romanorum sanguinem, primasque illustris pugnæ consequi victorias. His imperator erat Barbaris Caramuntes, senex jugulans divites, antiquorum dierum

Τοιαῦτα μὲν πράττοντες ἦσαν ἐν ζάλῃ Βίας τοσαύτης Κρῆτες, οἱ φευγόντες, Οἱ πολλάκις μὲν εἰσδραμόντες εἰς μάχην, Καὶ (92) πολλάκις πληγέντες ἐκ τοῦ σοῦ στόλου. 65 Τί δαὶ τὸ σὸν στράτευμα; τοῖς θαλαττίοις Οἱ μὲν τόποις ἔκειντο, καὶ γὰρ ἦν κρύος Βορρᾶ πνέοντος εἰσβολαῖς δυσπρακτοῖς· Οἱ δὲ κρατοῦντες τῶν πυλῶν τὰς ἐξόδους Τοὺς ἔνδον (93) ἐξέπληττον ἐν παραστάσει. 70 Μαθὼν δὲ τὴν φάλαγγα τῶν ἐναντίων Κοίλοις τόποις βαίνουσαν, ὡς δὲ θηρίον Θυμοῦ πνέουσαν, ἀλλὰ καὶ δειλανδρίας, Ἄρχων στρατηγὸς ἐν μάχῃ Νικηφόρος, Ἄπιστον εἶχε τοῦτο· καὶ παραυτίκα 75 Τάττει τὸ τάγμα τῶν Θρακηΐων (94) ὅλον Μαθεῖν τὸ πρᾶγμα· καὶ γὰρ ἦν τοῦτο ξένον Ἐν τῇ τοσαύτῃ συμφορᾷ πλέκειν δόλους Τοὺς ἐκκοπέντας ἑπτάκις (95) τῷ σῷ στόλῳ.
semper soles, pater enim et propugnaculum nostrum es in præliis. Ita ad spem erecta sine spe ignava illa generatio erupit congressura cum exercitu tuo, populus iniquus, infestus, cupiens sanguinem, genus improbum, Ismaelis orti ex famula. Talia quidem agebant in procella tantæ necessitatis Cretenses dispersi in fugam, qui sæpe excurrentes in prælium sæpe percussit sunt a tuis militibus. At quid exercitus tuus agebat? Littoreis alii quidem in locis jacebant, nam frigus erat Boreæ spirante vehementi impetu : alii autem custodientes portarum exitus astando terrebant eos, qui intus erant. Jam vero edoctus agmen hostium per anfractus incedere, ac ferarum instar iram spirare simul, et formidinem, supremus dux pugnæ Nicephorus vix illud potuit credere : sed statim ex Thracibus juvenibus totum agmen instruit, ut rem inquirat : et enim erat novum in tantis angustiis dolos nectere

Τούτοις στρατηγὸς ἦν ἀνὴρ ὁ τὸ ξίφος 80 Ποθῶν ὑπὲρ σοῦ, καὶ προτείνων εἰς μάχην, Καὶ μὴ δεδοικὼς τὴν τομὴν τῶν φασγάνων· Ὁ καὶ πρὶν εἰς ἄπληστον ἐμπεσὼν γένος Θάρσει μεγίστῳ, καὶ προθυμίᾳ ξένῃ, Καὶ δοὺς ἑαυτὸν εἰς σφαγὴν μονωτάτως. 85 Καὶ τοὺς ἀνίππους συνταράξας βαρβάρους· Ὃς, φεῦ, κρατηθεὶς τῇ τροπῇ Μακεδόνων, Καὶ πρὸς ξένην γῆν δέσμιος κατεσχέθη, Καὶ τοὺς ῥυποῦντας εἶχε πικροὺς δεσπότας. Οὗτος σταλεὶς ἔθεν ἐκ τοῦ πρῶτον λόχον, 90 Ἔρωτι πληγών, καρδίας ὑπερζέσει, Μέσον τὸ κοινὸν εὗρε βαρβάρων τέλος, Ὡς φύλλα βίψας τῶν μελῶν τὴν τετράδα (96). Ὡς γὰρ μέγιστος καὶ πολύχρονος λύκος 95 Ἔμπειρος ὢν ἁρπαξ τε καὶ ποιμηλάτης, Πεινῶν, ἑαυτὸν τῇ μονῇ τῶν ποιμνίων Θάρσει προπέμπει, καὶ καταβαίνει κύνας, Ἕως κρατηθῇ, καὶ σφαγῇ τοῖς ἀνδράσι, Οἱ πολλάκις ἔθρυξαν εἰς αὐτὸν μέγα. Οὕτως ἐκεῖνος ἐμπεσὼν στρατηγέτης 100 Ῥώμης κραταιός, καὶ μονωθεὶς εἰς μέσον, Ἔδειξεν οἵους ἄνδρας ἡ Ῥώμη τρέφει, Πολλῶν γὰρ αὐτῶν κύκλωθεν πεφραγμένων,
eos, qui ab exercitu tuo cæsi erant septies. Vix erat illis dux, qui gladium libenter pro te stringebat in prælio, neque reformidabat acies ensium : qui et antea genus inexplicabile invaserat audacia maxima, et alacritate mira, dederatque se ipsum in cædem unice, imperitosque equitandi confuderat barbaros, qui, heu! captus fuerat ob fugam Macedonum, vinctus ductus in terram extraneam, sordidos habuerat et iniquos dominos. Hic inde missus ad primas insidias vulnera cupiens ardenti pectore Barbarorum in medio communem invenit finem, dum quatuor membra projicere, tanquam folia. Quemadmodum enim ingens et senex lupus peritus raptor, et vastator gregum, esuriens, se ipsum in stationem ovium audacter introducit, et laniat canes, donec captus sit, cæsusque ab hominibus, qui sæpe infremuere in ipsum maxime : ita dux ille irruens Romæ fortis, solusque in medio hostium ox-

Πάντες τὸ κοινὸν ἐπτοοῦντο τοῦ τέλους· Ὡς γὰρ κεραυνὸν εἶχε χερσὶ τὸ ξίφος, 105 Κενῶν κατ’ ἐχθρῶν, καὶ καθαιμάττων φόνοις, Ὧι τοὺς μὲν ἐξέκοπτε τῶν ἐναντίων, Ἄλλους κατηνάγκαζε φεύγειν ἐντρόμους, Ἄλλους, θεὸν (97) κράζειν σε πείθων εἰς μάχην, Νεκροὺς ἐδείκνυ, καὶ πεφυρμένους κόνει. 110 Ὅμως δὴ πληγείς, καὶ πεσὼν ξένοις τόποις Πολλοὺς προεξένησεν τῶι στρατῶι πόνους. Καὶ δὴ δραμόντες ἱππόται, πεζόδρομοι Κρημνοὺς ἐκείνους καὶ φαραγγώδεις τόπους, Πάντας καθείρξαν, καὶ κατέκλεισαν μέσον, 115 Καὶ βρῶσιν εἰργάσαντο Κρῆτας ὀρνέων. Τοσοῦτον αὐτὸς εὐτύχησας ἐν μάχηι, Τοσοῦτον αὐτὸς ἐκράτησας βαρβάρων, Ἐκ τῆς ἄνω ῥοπῆς τε καὶ συνεργίας. Ἰδὼν δὲ τοῦτο καὶ φοβηθεὶς εὐθέως 120 Ὁ Καραμούντης ἐν χρόνοις ὁ δυσγέρων Βάλλει τὸν ὄγκον τοῦ φρονήματος κάτω, Φυγῆι δὲ χρᾶται πεζός· ἀλλὰ καὶ μόνος, Ὀπῆι δὲ μικρᾶι προστρέχει πετροστέγωι. Κτᾶται δ’ ἐκεῖθεν δυστυχῆ σωτηρίαν. 125 Ἔδει, γέρον, θανεῖν σε πολλῶν πρὸ χρόνων, Ἤπερ κλονεῖσθαι καὶ παρεισδύνειν πέτραις, Ὥσπερ λαγωὸς ἐκφυγὼν κυνηγέτας.
tendit quales viros Roma nutriat. Multis enim ex  ipsis eum circumdantibus, omnes communem extimebant finem, stringebat namque ensem, instar fulminis rotans hostes, et inficiens cædibus, quo alios quidem mactabat ex hostibus, alios cogebat trepidos aufugere, et alios, rogans te, ut advocares Deum ad pugnam, mortuos ostentavit et conspersos pulvere. Attamen vulneribus confossus extraneis in locis multos exercitui dolores intulit. Et jam accurrentes equites ac pedites hiantia illa et prærupta loca omnia cinxere, et incluserunt in medio, et Cretenses dedere in pastum avibus. Adeo in prælio fortuna usus es prospera, adeo domuisti Barbaros cooperante divino auxilio! Videns autem hoc, et extimescens statim Caramuntes senex miserrimus animi sui arrogantiam projicit, fugaque  utitur pedes, atque solus ad cavernam currit arctam in saxo excisam, ibique infelicem salitem invenit. Multos ante annos, senex, te mori oportuit, potius quam vexari, et includi inter saxa, veluti

Prima autem nocte consurgens per intima præcipitia montium festinabat anxius senex, Ameras pedes, aut potius quadrupes; cogebatur enim, ut suas manus detineret in pedum ministerium. Tuus autem, maxime Romane, exercitus ad mœnia conversus ea percutiebat alacriter gaudens hæc diruere et ad terram dejicere.

Ibi senes ruentes in fugam jaculis perestiebantur a tergero : ibi juvenes fugientes vestium discincta extensio cogebat fataliter, ut caderent, et impedimentum præbebat merito lex patria antiqua barbaris. Lex autem erat subditis a dacibus lata, et hoc ex jassione tua, o sceptrifer, ne dedecorarent stupris feminas, ne in non baptizatis puellis venerabile baptisma polluerent, et macularent milites tui. Consule Freins-
ON THE CONQUEST OF CRETE, ACROASIS I.
Sing, O Muse, of the rivers of blood of the Cretans. For when the army of the despot arrived, fortified by many swift merchant ships, the land of the barbarians groaned, and the neighboring sea turned away. The general who was sent, Nicephorus, knowing himself to be the leader of the battle, set up the phalanxes, all armed with swords; he set up the ranks, the tower-like squadrons. The shield shone, the sharpened sword [was ready] to tread correctly, and not to run badly, here put an end to the slaughters of Ilium, singing tragically of the rivers of blood of Crete. For when the army of the despot arrived, fortified by many swift ships, the land of the barbarians groaned, and the neighboring sea turned away. The general who was sent, Nicephorus, knowing himself to be the leader of the battle, set up the phalanxes, all armed with swords; he set up the ranks, the tower-like squadrons. The shield shone, the sharpened sword: the breastplate cast rays against the enemies; the held spear shone with terrible slaughter. But after he saw everyone saved from the waters and the greatest waves, standing in the middle, he opened his mouth to God: "Behold your army standing at the borders of the enemies, O Creator of created things; it has taken up the armor against your enemies, and it sets you forth, O glorious general, as one who loosens the bonds of ten thousand towers."
1002 The breastplate cast rays against the enemies; the held spear shone with terrible slaughter. But after he saw everyone saved from the waters and from the greatest storms, standing in the middle, the barbarian groaned, and the neighboring sea turned away. The general, however, who was sent, Nicephorus, seeing himself appointed as the leader of the battle, set up the phalanxes, all armed with swords, the ranks, and the cohorts proceeding in a square formation. The shield shone, and he opened his mouth to God: "Behold your army now standing at the borders of the enemies, O Creator of created things, it has taken up arms against your enemies, and it opposes you, so that you may dissolve the structures of thousands of towers, O glorious leader. Expand their hearts for battle, repel from [them]..."
VARIOUS READINGS. NOTE.
Theodosius here commemorates that the army was brought to Crete, as do other Greek writers. The uncertain continuator of Theophanes, who is believed to be blind, describes the number of these ships more diligently in the Life of Romanus the Younger, no. 10, page 297, with these words: "For there were two thousand ships with Greek fire, one thousand dromons, and three hundred and seven cargo ships carrying provisions and weapons of war." Simeon Magister and Logothete also recounts an equal number of ships in Romanus the Younger, no. 3, page 498, and differs only in that he counts the cargo ships up to three hundred and sixty. He is commended in verses, who, after he led the army into Crete, arranged the soldiers in order, and ordered each to stand in his own place, lest they be oppressed by the Hagarenes if attacked unexpectedly: which Simeon Magister also attributes to the praise of Nicephorus in Romanus the Younger, no. 5, page 498, writing thus: "Nicephorus the Domestic, having set sail from the Queen of Cities, and reaching Phygela, was concerned that the entire fleet should be with him at the same place, and should anchor." Thus, Nicephorus the Domestic, setting sail from Byzantium and coming to Phygela, strove for this most of all, and exerted every care that the whole fleet should be joined in one line and should call. I was explaining, and I understood "tower-like squadrons" as those ambulatory towers which were in use among the ancients to hasten the conquest of towers. Hesychius also interprets the word "tower" in that sense, writing: "Tower: a fighting instrument," namely a war instrument, about which they discuss more fully in Hero of Byzantium, *On War Machines*, chapter 13, and Vegetius, book IV, chapter 17, where Stuvechius also published an image of this war machine. But since "tower," as the same Hesychius notes, is also "a rank of soldiers in a square," a column of soldiers in a quadrangular form, and it occurs more frequently used by Greek writers in that sense, it should perhaps be considered that Theodosius spoke of this very column. Eustathius on book IV of the Iliad, page 469 of the Roman edition, brings a distinction that existed between "squadron" and "tower," and says that the first column was composed of 40 men, but the other consisted of three hundred and sixty men: "A squadron is a rank of forty men... a tower is of three hundred and sixty: this rank is thickened in a quadrangular way." A squadron is a battle line, or a group of forty men... a tower is a turreted battle line, or a square one, a column of three hundred and sixty men, so that this column is dense and compacted in a quadrangular form. Since, however, the same learned grammarian adds that those names were not always used so precisely, but were often used by Homer in a broader way, the translation of Cornelius is not to be disapproved, who interpreted "tower-like squadrons" as one and the same column. For it is possible that Theodosius used the word "tower-like" here in the same sense in which Homer said "tower-like" in Iliad, book XII, v. 45, writing: "But they, having arranged themselves like a tower, stood opposite." To which words the aforementioned Eustathius notes on page 892 of the Roman edition: "And 'having arranged themselves like a tower' [means] instead of 'having arranged themselves in the manner of a tower'; and 'tower' is now said to be a military arrangement in a quadrangular form, arranged according to a brick." Those words "having arranged themselves like a tower" signify "disposed like a tower"; but "tower" is now said by the poet to be a military instruction in a quadrangular form, arranged according to a brick. ...due to carelessness: but it should be read "sharpened," as Cornelius has already seen well. ...of the writers, he mentions the prayers which Theodosius now narrates that Nicephorus poured out to God. It is likely, however, that this was not written by the poet entirely for the sake of flattering Nicephorus, since the uncertain continuator of Theophanes in the Life of Romanus the Younger, no. 11, page 298, writes that Nicephorus was feared by the Hagarenes because of his "vigilance and wisdom, and faith in God, and justice." The Christian people of the Cretans [destroyed] the towers and walls of...
ON THE CONQUEST OF CRETE, ACROASIS I.
And of the wife: let her not fear this. Let him only hold the shield with entanglements; let him only eagerly extend the sharp spear; let him only appear armed as a father; let him only not insult the power of Rome.” Having winged the servants with these words, the man faithful in battles, Nicephorus, sent out lions as if mountain-bred, shaking all Cretan wickedness. And since all were instead of wild beasts, spread out from there and here like a plague, not indeed carelessly, or without a general. For he himself, girded in the midst of the horsemen, a long-mouthed sharpened sword, now striking in a strange way, now again casting the whip of fire upon the houses.
1006 It was to see a pitiful lament and flight then, and a bitter wonder of contrary calamities. For a multitude of men and women lay in one dust, miserably slaughtered. There children, hastening to slaughter, fell to the ground by the breaking of their feet. There old men, bowed by time, slipped and fell in the way, and the sword dyed the Phoenician hair, which time had dyed white; there women, from their arms, threw infants to the ground, and [the danger] stirred up a lack of affection for their offspring.

### [LATIN TRANSLATION AND NOTES]
...he shall have, assuming the name of Despot hereafter, [with] sword, now striking he produced wondrous works, now he himself shall bear the care of him, and of his sons, and of his wife: let him not fear this in any way. Let him only oppose the shield in the encounters, let him only alacritously extend the sharp spear, let him only show himself a fatherly soldier, let him only not disgrace the empire of Rome. When the man faithful in battles, Nicephorus, had thus added wings to the soldiers, he sent them out like mountain lions to devastate all Cretan impropriety, not without a plan of battle, or without a leader; for he himself riding in the midst, armed with a huge and sharp sword, casting the whip of fire upon the houses. But after all had gone out against the beasts, spreading rapidly like a plague here and there, you could see heavy mourning, and flight, and the straits of the enemies horrible to behold. For a crowd of men and women lay in one dust, miserably slaughtered. There children hastening to slaughter fell to the ground trampled by feet; there old men bowed by age were cut down meeting [the enemy] in their path, and the sword dyed the purple hair, which age had dyed white: there women from their bosom...
VARIED READINGS. 97. κρατείτω in note, or κρατεῖν C. see Annot. 99. Ser. πατρικῶς with C. πατρικὸς cod., which reading Fogginius wrongly defends in the Annot. 113. Perhaps συμφορᾶς. 116. Perhaps πρὸς φυγήν. 121. εἶχεν C.
NOTES. (33) It seems to be written thus in the codex. Cornelius thought it should be corrected to κρατείτω: but, in my opinion, nothing should be changed. [Cornelius doubts whether κρατείτω (thus, not κρατείνω) was in the codex, or κρατείτω. He exhibited the former in the context; Fogginius the latter. Whence it appears that some things in this annotation are confused. F. J.] (34) Cornelius published πατρικῶς, and translated it, in the manner of a father armed. The codex has πατρικὸς, which word I rendered into Latin as "fatherly soldier," and we accepted that reading the more willingly because it coheres better with the following verse. For since Nicephorus says here that each one strives to show himself a fatherly soldier, he adds, nor does he disgrace the Roman empire. ὡπλισμένος is used here for ὁπλίτης, just as Thucydides sometimes writes τοὺς ἐν ὅπλοις for the same reason. (35) Here the poet expresses with words the address of Nicephorus to the soldiers for engaging with the enemies, just as fear added wings to Turnus in Virgil, Aen. VIII, v. 224, to escape the anger of Hercules. (36) The poet best expresses the virtue of the Greek soldiers in these words. For Greek writers, when they wish to indicate that someone was killed by an enemy while fighting strenuously, say "the work of the enemy," "the work of war," and "of the sword": but old men, women, infants, and other non-combatants, whom the enemy kills without any trouble and as if doing something else, they say were made a "parergon" (a side-task), to signify the ease of the slaughter. Thus Pausanias writes in the Phocics, book X, chap. 27, that Priam, consumed by old age, while he was killed by Neoptolemus, a strenuous and brave youth, was a "parergon" to him, that is, killed by him without any labor. For the same reason, therefore, Theodosius, wishing to imply the fortitude of the Greeks, says that Nicephorus sent out his soldiers οὐ παρέργως, not to be overcome by an easy task, as they were rightly instructed, so that they could not be conquered by the Barbarians. [οὐ μὴν παρέργως, not indeed carelessly, and with the care that the matter required. Cf. to Philostratus the Younger, Imagg. p. 613. F. J.] (37) The poet graphically describes the slaughter that the Barbarians received as soon as Nicephorus brought his army to Crete. Leo the Deacon deals with this first conflict of the Greeks with the Cretan Saracens, where he says: Then the Roman leader Nicephorus, having distributed the line of battle in a triple column, and crowded far with shields and spears, sounding the trumpet of war, when he had ordered the trophy of the cross to go before, rushed with face turned against the Barbarians: and a heavy battle having been joined, and missiles flying like hail, it could not be that the Barbarians could bear the shower of Roman arrows any longer: but rather turning their backs, and with the phalanx broken, making their course as fast as they could, they retreated to their own stronghold. The Romans following behind gave them an immense slaughter. Such, indeed, was the first aggression and battle that had a favorable end for the Romans. See Pagi, Crit. in Ann. Bar. vol. III, p. 874. [Ἐπεὶ δὲ πάντες ἦσαν ἀντὶ θηρίων is wrongly translated "went out against the beasts." The meaning is that the Roman soldiers rushed upon the enemies no differently than wild beasts and poured themselves out everywhere like a pestilence into the hostile land. Cf. below v. 208. F. J.] (38) [τῇ ποδῶν περικλάσει is translated: trampled by feet. Rather, in that haste, when they were fleeing—for πρὸς φυγήν should be read instead of πρὸς σφαγήν—they fell, with the strength of their feet broken; which is λύτο γούνατα in Homer. F. J.] (39) So great a slaughter received struck fear into the women, that they even forgot their natural affection for their children because of the imminent danger. "Danger stirred up a lack of affection for offspring," so that we might better express the force of this Greek phrase, we translated: Danger had caused, so that they would forget even natural affection for their children because of the imminent danger.

...when the general had stationed the crowd so that they might be dragged to slaughter, their fellow-soldiers could not bear the event of bitter pain, but with doors opened they flew out like sparrows, and rushing in troops to the trench they were violently crossing it, but struck from the front they were hastening to retreat. Therefore, when there was an image of flight, and of fear, and of terrible dread, and a headlong tumult, the trench itself was a tomb of the enemies, and an open house of slaughter. For there were for them as an impediment even the places which they had prepared for their own safety, so that from places also devoid of sense they might learn their own faith worthy of insults. What prophet, now asked before the battle, would have said to the barbarians digging the trench, that they were digging a tomb for their children, not a rampart for themselves?
VARIED READINGS.
With these words you were admonishing [them] concerning the danger: “Why, not fearing the general, O horseman, do you lie in strange places during the heat of battle, where consternation by manifest danger breathes burning impulses against us? Endure a little something, and with helmet imposed stand for religion, and for the laws: shake off sleep, and seize the shield, lest the general of the battle know the matter.” Thus you were driving away sloth from the soldier, if ever it was in your servants. But when the dawn had begun to herald light with a white garment expelling the shadows, and had adorned the air with bright garments, you would see the earth whitening, and the herald of battle running through your armies, and leading the phalanx from the high wall, which was suddenly about to rush upon the enemies. There, when the general had stationed the whole crowd of captives, that they might be dragged to slaughter, their fellow-soldiers could not bear the event of bitter pain, but with doors opened they flew out like sparrows, and rushing in troops to the trench they were violently crossing it, but struck from the front they were hastening to retreat. Therefore, when there was an image of flight, and of fear, and of terrible dread, and a headlong tumult, the trench itself was a tomb of the enemies, and an open house of slaughter. For there were for them as an impediment even the places which they had prepared for their own safety, so that from places also devoid of sense they might learn their own faith worthy of insults. What prophet, now asked before the battle, would have said to the barbarians digging the trench, that they were digging a tomb for their children, not a rampart for themselves?
[Notes]
(60) Something is missing in this verse for the meter.
(61) Earth whitening, as if illuminated by the dawn.
(62) That wall, or rampart, built by the protection of his army, I am certain is designated here. Hear Leo the Deacon...
(63) Cornelius published "small," and judged it should be corrected...
(64) Hesychius interprets this word as to prepare, which word for arming and instructing an army does not occur among Greek writers. Here, however, it seems to be used with a more peculiar meaning for soldiers being called to war...
(65) A truly beautiful stratagem of Nicephorus, of placing the barbarian captives in the front of the phalanx, so that they might be killed in the sight of their own citizens, and even thus the Saracens might be stirred to go out to battle.
(66) This is the trench which the Saracens had dug for their own protection, as Leo the Deacon narrates thus: "There were also two trenches, led as wide as possible, and deep, which provided a fortification on all sides."
(67) Thus the codex: and so also at the edge of the page.
THEODOSIUS THE DEACON
[LATIN TRANSLATION]
...the sky was covering with clouds. Air, now speak, bear witness, do not hesitate, the woven warp of your garment, by which protected you repelled the harm, weaving deceits from hidden ambushes, from which those raising their eyebrows, rapacious dogs, were cast down from the battlements. But when for eight days the iron rain falling had not ceased, but fate was bringing the missile and the quiver, he moved up rams, and tortoise-engines, and slings throwing stones, and terrible compositions against their unjoined ladders. When therefore the town-openers, not bearing those new punishments, knew not where to turn, your beloved servant Nicephorus arranges the crowd and adds new penalties. For he ordered the heads of the slain enemies to be thrown by slings like missiles, so that the thin breadth of the ether might rejoice, stained by the slaughter of the barbarians, and the sinful people might know that even their own limbs were for their punishment. They were throwing inside instead of stones the heads, which...
[VARIOUS READINGS AND NOTES]
(83) In the edition of Cornelius, "aer" (air) is read, but "age" (come) is found in the codex. (84) Namely the tortoise, which he describes metaphorically as a garment. (85) Cornelius omitted this article: for which reason... (86) The damage which the barbarians began to inflict upon the Greeks, against them was yielding: for the Greeks, enclosed in the tortoise, from the openings of the machine itself, deliberately cut in safe places for this purpose, were sending missiles against them, which were harming them greatly as they stood in the open above the towers and walls. (87) Thus the codex; Cornelius had read otherwise, but it has been well conjectured that it should be corrected thus. (88) I think "kera" (fate) is put for "kiron" (wax), or by an error of the copyist, or rather by a fault of the age, by which the elegance of the Greek language had in great part perished. (89) Nicephorus used these machines to attack the Saracen walls, as Leo the Deacon also narrates, writing thus: "This having been perceived, the commander brought up stone-throwers as quickly as possible, and ordered the Barbarians to be attacked with a rain of stones. To these he also applied a siege machine (the Romans call it a ram): and with the stone-throwers hurling heavy weights of stones, the Barbarians were easily repressed: and meanwhile, with the ram moved to the walls and shaking them with greater force, not a few men went under the trench." (90) Namely, siege tortoises, called by them different names, and therefore also named differently by him. This machine was supported by four wheels, so that it could be carried more easily where there was need, and was covered on the outside with the raw hides of animals for its own protection. It contained soldiers for undermining the walls, and then a balanced ram was swung to shatter them. Vitruvius described it excellently in book x, chapter 19. (91) These are ladders which were compactable, or solutible. They were called "klimakas pektas," that is, foldable ladders, by Appian in the Iberica, and more to our point "dialutas," that is, solutible, by Plutarch in the Life of Aratus, where he says that Aratus, about to besiege Sicyon, had hidden them by throwing them into grain measures. (92) We restored this article omitted by Cornelius from the codex. (93) A terrible stratagem indeed to terrify the enemy, devised by Nicephorus, namely, the throwing of the severed heads of the barbarians by means of slings, so that they might fall struck even by their own fellow-citizens. None of the historians whose writings have reached us recorded such a stratagem of Nicephorus: but perhaps Leo the Deacon had made mention of it, who had recorded more stratagems of Nicephorus in this Cretan conquest. (94) Correct this better for "leptonemon." (95) The words "tes hamartias" seem to be joined with...
ON THE CONQUEST OF CRETE, ACROASIS II.
And their brothers, and one dying in battle
Was a murderer through paternal slaughter.
For that man, having been released from the prevailing carnal blindness,
And having learned what is advantageous,
Being urged to fight alongside the worshippers of Christ,
Was a fatal dart to the false-worshippers.
As the city therefore held those bodies
Full of pollutions, those who were of my kin
Cried out thus with the clamor of the barbarians:
"Seepph, echimat, ischarop, and the rhasan,
Sermet, midene, and chaet ipphesane."
But the cry was according to their ancestral custom,
But the slaughter was found by you for the Cretans.
Now the one holding the command of their army was
An old Emir, pale, full of labors,
Small, bald, cunning, surpassing the one from Pylos
In honey-flowing words.
But the words did not help him at all,
Even if they were straightforward to attack your power;
Nevertheless he dared, for he was a commander
Feeding himself on empty hopes.
He makes a plan, and calls the elders,
His companions in time, to a council.
Having sat there and groaned from the depths,
He let out a voice mixed with his labors:
"Which of you, men, has fire in his heart,
Burning, flaming, as the phalanx of Rome
Burns me, shaking my lordship?
Whose children have received the final harm
From the sharpened sword?
Whose field is being devoured by the seething
Fire that has come upon it, the bitter punishment?
Let that man advise us now,
1022 Giving the best opinion instead of strength.
But, if it pleases, let us station horsemen,
Three times seven to the fifty-fold attackers,
Then thirty thousand foot-soldiers,
All iron-clad, dark-skinned to the sight,
So that they may strike the opponents with terror,
Appearing to them from the gates beyond expectation."
Thus he who had previously been stubbornly spread out,
While haranguing, was unfortunately tightened.

68. θανεῖν C. The same omits εἷς. Both corrected by F. from the same, it seems. 75. Write ξύναιμοι. 80. σφαγὴ σοι C. 92. Perhaps τοῖς στόνοις. 93. ἡμῶν C. 107. Perhaps ἐκτὸς τῶν πυλῶν. 108. ἀφθαδώς C.
NOTES.
(96) I read these words thus, which seem faulty and incomplete in the first [edition]. Furthermore, the perturbation and clamor of the Saracens at that time in Crete, excited among them by the novelty of the most cruel matter, Theodosius expresses in their own language and dialect. The words seem Turkish, but of very difficult interpretation, because they are obsolete and perhaps expressed in Greek letters not without the errors of a scribe. If anything should emerge in the meantime, we will provide it at the end.
(97) [Sense: The townspeople raising a clamor, they were using their ancestral speech, but that method of killing the Cretans was your invention, therefore recent and unusual.]
(98) [The text omits the translation of the Greek words.]
(99) Amir, an Arabic word, which signifies lord or master, from which, both among the Greeks and among the Latins of a later age, many words were derived to signify various dignities which they held among the Saracens and Turks with some authority. Moreover, from this word the title "Admiral" is in honor among us, by which the Sicilians, in imitation of the Saracens, called the supreme commanders of fleets.
(1) The Pylian old man, namely Nestor, whom the poet describes as the most eloquent of the Greeks.
(2) Elders equal in time.
(3) Cornelius edited it less correctly and without any sense.
(4) Curupas certainly strove to extend his empire, since the Barbarians, as witnessed by the anonymous continuator of Theophanes in Rom. Jun. no. 7, page 296: "they committed many terrible and evil things and captivities every day into the land of the Romans, from the time they ravaged this great island," no damages...

110 And indeed, having campaigned and marshaled his entire force, the dragon gathered serpents from their dens. But knowing that they were all walking the paths of cowardice, or choosing slaughter, he stood and urged on those evil workers:
115 "Let us hasten in delight, O horsemen, to the battle of ten thousand swords that has now appeared to us. Let us go to the sword for the sake of our children, for our wives, for our friends, and for our fatherland. THEODOSIUS THE DEACON
Great noise, and fear, the father of trembling, and all things terrible; but for your generals, order, silence, gravity, and eagerness. One, therefore, of the generals, a branch of a noble root, a man mighty in the casting of a javelin, slow to flee, and swift to pursue, spoke sweet words in the midst of the army: "Supreme commander, why, having left the master, did you not, like a cook in the culinary art, mix him into the exhortation of these men, so that the sweet, crown-bearing nectar might become a seasoning for your commands? He, admonishing us, permitting us, and extending his hand to all, was a benefactor. Let us remember his honey-flowing words, and the tables, and then the gifts. Remember, and let us die, men, as is fitting, for the sake of the family of the most pious king. Only let the scepter of the monarchy be extended, and let Romanus alone rule over all the earth."
But when the myriad-strong multitude of barbarians rushed from the gates, like a cloud of the south, threatening a roar and showing forth destruction, your men, turning into a feigned flight, devised a trap for the Cretan-slaying. And all, having gone out from the long tower, and having rushed forth outside the gates, paid the penalties worthy of their faith. For from four military ambushes, a wave arose, and having attacked fiercely, it began to generate a winter before the battle; and for the enemies there was calamity, tumult, and groaning, but for your peoples, a triumphant joy. When, therefore, they stood in the space between, seeing their exit narrowed, they, the authors of their own slaughter, struck immediately, but were struck more; and having their flight blocked—for the harm was disturbing them on all sides—they stood in one place of burial. Who among the tragic poets will cry out the slaughters, and the punishment of each one’s massacre?
VARIOUS READINGS.
176. Perhaps "noise," a clamor stirred up to strike terror. 188. "Us" omitted in G. (18) Thus I have restored this pronoun to its place in the ode of the manuscript. (19) Thus it should be well corrected; I read "be extended" in the note. (20) Cornelius preferred to read "rain," but it is nothing to be changed; for "roar" is well used for a storm and tempest. (21) Cornelius wrongly reads "clashes." "Ambushes" is correct. It is established that Greek writers used "ambush" everywhere to designate military traps. Moreover, Eustathius in his Commentary on Homer, page 932, Roman edition, contends that "ambush" is derived from "to hide," writing: "Whence also the man of the people calls an ambush a hiding; for those who do not stand stretched out are entirely hidden, but necessarily contracting themselves while crouching, so as not to be seen." Wherefore also "ambush" is commonly called a hiding; for they are entirely hidden who stand compressed, and necessarily, while they come together, contract themselves so as not to be seen. More stratagems of this kind are reported by Polyaenus, book ii, chapter 1, number 27; book v, chapter 10, number 2; chapter 16, number 2; book viii, chapter 3, number 2, and elsewhere.

For one of them, struck in the kidneys, suffered vertigo, which he did not understand, and before he had learned the turnings of dancers, he was an unwilling dancer, having been struck by a javelin. Another, deprived of his shorn head, lay prone, stretched out by the army, and moving his feet unhappily, he wished to swim in a flood of blood. Another, struck in the dry spine, was bending over, unwillingly worshipping your fleet. Another, dead and with his feet cut off, lay neglected for the trampling of horses. 230 Another, thrust in his insatiable belly, held with his hands the tangle of his intestines, and he who before was judged a strong fighter, was an unskilled butcher of entrails (22). Thus did the sons of Hagar exchange their armor for other calamitous arts, and everyone dying had as his punishment a wicked art for the destruction of his life. There was, therefore, among them a Cretan knight, giant-like, who, having taken his shield and sword, drove his horse against the general of the battle, wishing to attack him.
1030 But Justice (23) did not allow him to succeed; for Nicephorus, who was in command, struck him below the loins, in the middle of the navel. 245 And the dust (24) held his dead body, but the broad (25) sword held his greatest glory. Thus did those who were hemmed in suffer evil, justly, from the killers on this side and that; they, the ministers of lawless deeds. If any of them was intent on flight, he had in front of him barriers (26) and a lethal sword, and the general Nicephorus everywhere striking and shouting to the commanders: "Let us strike, men, these life-destroying wolves." Homer, you who write of the battle in poetic words—being far away, yet writing as if present—who labor much in empty things, and recount all the toils of the Greeks in vain: 260 Come here, and proclaim with the weight of words the glory of the army's strength, and the great groaning of the ancient, hostile, slave-born (27) enemies. For you will have in us the truth as a model, just as you have in the Achaeans the digression as a model. For there was no one there who fled from the battle, not even if the barbarian had a winged nature.

He who closes, restrains, opens, and sends forth the many-mouthed outpourings of the abyss, He extends the borders of your power, O son of the King, and strengthens the fleet, and does all things, and destroys cities. And who among the writers of prose, O King, will be able to praise you according to your merit to such a degree, even if there were another among the Stagirites, or Plato, or the one who rules Athens, Demosthenes, or he who strikes the much-celebrated lyre?
1032 The writer of the present day begins, but the goads of timidity prick him, and constrain his speech. Do you grant the boldness, open wide the mouth, teach us not to be silent easily, when such a calamity has befallen the barbarians. Widen the throat for frankness; for it is constricted when approaching your labors; the hand trembles, the mind is often restrained, and it shrinks, and is terrified, and wishing to walk in your Christ-imitating ways, it runs about fearfully like a hare. For you, having attained victory everywhere, have benefited the common good, and have increased it alone. You have pushed the phalanx of the adversaries with your labors into the chasm of impassable places. You have filled the cities not only with spoils, but you have filled them with peace, and much glory. Let the order of angels above rejoice, let the assembly of mortals below dance.

ON THE CONQUEST OF CRETE, ACROASIS III.
It was not Hercules who saved the Phrygians from the sea monster, but the Despot who saved the world from murderous slaughter. It was not Meleager who saved his fatherland from the fire, but Romanos who has just now saved the entire creation. What profit was it long ago for your sons, Aloeus, to craft a great bronze jar for Ares? For it was not a stepmother who moved this against the Cretan enemies, but the Despot, having unsealed the jar. For it was not fitting, O Sun, while your scepter adorned the monarchy of the crown, for him to be confined and remain in a prison; but rather that you should hold the shield with him, and stain the earth with the blood of the barbarians. And when the phalanx of the Cretans was slaughtered, as a hare falling among hunters is destroyed, the earth was delivered from many calamities, and was freed from countless pollutions; and every Scythian lay dead upon the ground, desired by dogs, and by wives, and by children. Ignorant of the outcomes of all these things, you yourself, O General, were again gathering your cares within, like an army, in your heart, until the letter from your subjects arrived, filled with great joy. And having learned everything, and having clapped your hands—when nature does not have a prayer to speak—you gave it to God; but perhaps, O King, you sang that of the prophet: "O Lord, I cry out loudly, my Lord, do not be silent at all; for if you should be silent, I shall be like those in the pit."

It was not Hercules who saved the Phrygians from the sea monster, but the Despot who saved the world from murderous slaughter: the earth was snatched from miseries and purged of infinite filth: and every Scythian lay dead upon the ground, desired by dogs, and by wives, and by children. Because you yourself, O Emperor, were ignorant of the outcomes of all these things, you were tormented by new cares in your mind regarding your army, until a little later you received letters from your servants filled with great joy. Having been informed of all things, and clapping your hands, you poured out prayers to God which nature is not able to utter: but perhaps, O King, you sang that of the prophet: "O Lord, I cry out loudly, my Lord, do not be silent at all; for if you should be silent, I shall be like those in the pit."
JC43 THEODOSIUS THE DEACON
Let these, O King, march together against us, your subjects; the city has no fear, possessing your armor. Myths proclaim the labors of Hercules, they exalt him beyond the truth, they cry out much, and they terrify me, and perhaps they attribute the greatest things to him deservedly. Yet he conquered, or he leveled cities. For you might say many things; but such a one, when? There was no other king of ancient Rome, there was no other like the glorious despot. I have read many books of many historians; Plutarch, extolling the power of the Romans (I knew him well—but he often steals the vanity and introduces the weight, he exalts the small and pulls down the greater). I did not find Dion the cicada, the wise lyre, as brilliant as you in generalship; I did not find another second general; I did not find another Roman counselor. I did not find another; nor the son of Philip, whom Plutarch holds as the first in battles. So much have you been raised by the cooperation of God; so much have you surpassed others in battles; so much have you terrified enemies with the sword.
ACROASIS IV.
Just now, O powerful light, ruler of the inhabited world, while your army was laboring in the encounter under the weight of iron, and toil, and care, in great winter, and unspeakable frosts; the first dogs of the Cretans from outside, no longer lords of the land, and leaders of phalanxes then—for they themselves inhabited cliffs and ravine-filled places—having been driven out from there by the sword, struck by the onslaught and the blow of the cold, and not yet renouncing their own times, together against us (for they were against fellow-citizens) they rushed from the mountains to a single slaughter.

ON THE CONQUEST OF CRETE, ACROASIS IV.
Just as the mountain-bred wild race of goats, hares, and timid deer, deprived of food by the bitter winter, descends from the high, ice-cold places and walks through hollow lands, searching for food and grassy spots; so the base children of sin ran to the wave-beaten places of the sea, to hidden shelters and valleys. And just as the greedy race of wolves among beasts, hastening to find satiety for their bellies, advance with rage and the emptiness of their stomachs, gaping wide their mouths before they reach their prey; so the race of those slave-born men, constrained by your flocks, O Chief Shepherd, rose up against them and sharpened their swords, eager to devour flesh instead of food, and to drink the warm blood of the Romans, and to seize first the brilliant victories of the battle.
The leader of these barbarians was the young dragon of the ancient days, he who had in mind to hold the reins of the Cretans for the whole time of his life. He was then preparing to cast a multitude of ten thousand into the slaughter. And when he saw them all down from the mountains, sword-bearing, equestrian, and foot-soldiers, he stood and exhorted them, and urged them toward the swords, setting up phalanxes and young phalanx-leaders, fencing the surroundings with circles from the outside, crying out and shouting such things to the barbarians: "Great, men, is our necessity, great the turmoil, the storm, and the labor, but our salvation is quickly at hand, if only we are willing, found by our hands. Let us go against the enemies, let us not fear swords, let us stand against them, let us not be terrified by the spear, and having pushed them all into the sea, we shall set up a trophy of slaughter here." And all immediately said as if with one mouth: "Let us die, if we must, today with our fathers..."

ON THE CONQUEST OF CRETE, ORATION V.
For which he filled the earth with the pollutions of slaughter;
For which he existed as the inventor of sufferings.
This [the earth], the glorious, the sweet branch of Rome,
The most serene, the slayer of the barbarians,
The most venerable and persecutor of Syria,
He conquered, as it was sending forth fiery fevers against us.
O great strength!
O the double-mouthed, three-conched [shields], double-mouthed swords,
And three-conched arrows, all-bronze helmets,
Which wrought the slaughter of the bloodthirsty,
Or which stopped the daily murders,
Which the sea held, more piratical than any.
O the whirling of the stone-throwing slings,
The snorting of horses, the sharpness of javelins,
And of the sword, and breastplates, the tension of the shield,
Which leveled the earth that was being barbarized,
Which the Master gave instead of medicines,
And saved the world from tyranny.
O the spirit that navigated the myriad ships,
And the harmless guide of the Master, having been seen.
Lament, O all you land of Syria, now,
Having your own leader of the outcry,
Your own wanderer, not a prophet, Moameth,
Whose tunic, stained with myriad shameful deeds,
The sons of the bondwoman hide;
They hide it as if it were spotless, though it is stained;
They hold it as if it were undefiled, though it is polluted.
For it was necessary, being from the defilement of pollutions,

[Latin Translation]
...is, since he filled the world with unjust slaughters, which overturned the earth made barbarian, which since he existed as the inventor of vices. This [earth], the glorious, the sweet germ of Rome, the most serene, the tamer of barbarians, the most august, and persecutor of Syria, which was sending forth fiery heats against us, he conquered: O great power! O trifid bosses, two-edged swords, and triple-pointed missiles, and brazen helmets, which accomplished the slaughter of the homicides, and daily killed those whom the sea held as the worst pirates. O the whirling of the stone-throwing slings, the snorting of horses, the sharpness of weapons, and swords, and breastplates, and the tension of the shield, which the Master gave instead of medicines, to heal the world from tyranny! O the breeze governing infinite ships, and providing yourself as a safe companion, Master! Lament now, O whole land of Syria, having your prince, your deceiver, not a prophet, Moameth, as a companion of your groans; whose garment, stained with infinite filth, the sons of the bondwoman hide; they hide it as if it were without stain, they hold it as if it were undefiled, though it is polluted. For it was necessary, as they were instituted in filth, to call the scoundrels born of barbarian stock immaculate.

...proceeds with the words: "he conquered this." NOTES.
...better amends [it as] "roof," but by what reasoning and in what sense, I do not understand. For my part, I would correct it to "having covered," so that "he had covered" might indicate he suffered, or permitted, for even in this sense the verb "to cover" occurs among Greek writers. Cornelius corrects from this passage, led by the reasoning that the verb "to conquer" is usually constructed with the genitive. Truly, although more rarely, Greek writers also attribute the accusative to it, and the fact that Theodosius constructed it thus is proven by the participle "sending forth," which Cornelius would otherwise have had to correct as well.
...built with three arches by the emperors, is often mentioned by Byzantine writers, whose authorities Du-Fresne cites in his *Christian Description of Constantinople*, book 11, section 4, number 11; and book 4, section 7, number 1. Truly, Theodosius uses this name in a completely different sense, who seems to have used it transferred to arms to signify what Polybius, book 6, page 400, called "iron boss," namely the boss of a shield, or that iron spike which protruded in the middle of the shield and was triangular. Not dissimilarly, more recent Greeks use the word "three-conched" to designate a triangle.
...Cornelius corrected, which he inserted into the text, not from an old [source], but a word corrupted by a bovine scribe, while he noted that he had received it poorly described.
...Theodosius affirms that the Turks pursued [them]), among those who have written about the life of Moameth and the dogmas of the Turks, there is no mention. Wherefore I would believe that either this word has a defect, or it must be explained in a sense other than that of a garment. Ecclesiastical writers, among whom are Theodoret, *Questions on Genesis* 39, page 34; St. Athanasius, *Questions to Antiochus* 55; St. Gregory Nazianzen, *Oration* 38, page 619; and Origen, as testified by St. Epiphanius, both in *Heresy* 64, page 229, and in the *Ancoratus*, page 494, discussing those leather tunics with which God covered Adam and Eve after the sin, transferred the word "tunic," which the sacred pages use to designate flesh and body, in a sense certainly less obvious. Consult, if you wish, their authorities in Suicer's *Ecclesiastical Thesaurus*. Therefore, what if Theodosius used the word in the same sense? That the body of Moameth is buried in a small shrine built over it in Medina, so that it cannot be seen by anyone, and that the Turks pursue it there with the greatest and absolutely divine honors, is known to all. Hence it happens that the poet deservedly mocks these infamous worshipers, who venerated the body of Moameth as the remains of a pure and holy man, while they ought to have devoted it as polluted and execrable because of the manner of life most basely led by him.
...by a printer's error.
...which is washed by the African sea, the African Saracens, aided by the arts and deceits of Euphemius, invaded the island and made it their own, under the Emperor Michael the Stammerer, in the year 828. See among others Cedrenus.

THEODOSIUS THE DEACON
Dance, O earth, dance, as much of you as belongs to the Romans,
As you have been fortunate in the crowns of palaces,
As you have expanded into the ancestral breadths,
As you have mastered those who were formerly masters.
Would that you might rise from the tomb, Constantine,
Would that you might look upon how the affairs of Syria stand.
You would have recognized a son, like Philip, a horseman;
And you would have said to him, "O child, seek an empire,
For the whole earth is not enough for you, such is your power."
For what a groan of lamentations was that of Crete!
What a tearing apart, when they saw the naked Roman swords
From within the towers,
When they saw small children and mothers,
Some with their hands twisted behind them in ropes,
Some defiled with blood and dust,
Some laid out in rows for slaughter.
There children embraced their fathers, and fathers
Their children, and together the sword
Drenched them in torrents of blood.
There was no prophet who was strong, nor a sword—
For this [sword] is honored by the enemies as a god.
There, old men who had rushed into flight
Were struck from behind by javelins.
There, the loose extension of their garments
Caused the fleeing youths to slip and fall,
And the ancient ancestral law provided a stumbling block
Rightly for the barbarians.
For there was a law for the subjects from the generals,
And this was from your command, O scepter-bearer:
Not to commit wanton outrage against women,
Lest the venerable baptism be stained in unbaptized maidens,
And your fleet be defiled.
You cared so much for your servants, O King,
That your words anticipated even the smallest details,
And you humbled yourself even to these for the sake of your friends.
For every man who holds a shield is a friend to you,
And every man who draws a sword in battle.
ON THE EXCERPTS CONCERNING EMBASSIES. 050 to make a public proclamation of his own elevation to the empire. For the Roman emperors of that time were accustomed, as soon as they had attained the empire, to announce it to the King of the Persians through envoys, as Theophylactus also testifies. Which custom Justin also wished to observe at the beginning of his reign. that he might redeem the peace. Wherefore he patiently endured that the legions should be diminished and [text corrupted], scribe, [text corrupted], from the treasury, as if they would be of no use thereafter. For whereas the entire forces of the Roman empire ought to have consisted of 645 thousand soldiers, they barely consisted of 150 thousand at that time. So greatly had the forces of the Romans been cut down and diminished by the negligence of the prince, which the prefects of the praetorium soon abused for their own gain, partly defrauding the soldier of his pay, and partly paying it late. These things are from Agathias. Ibid. B. For not long after. This first embassy of the Avars is said to have entered Constantinople in the 30th year of Justinian in book xvi of the *Historia Miscella*. But Justinian departed from life in the 40th year of his reign. But I think the year of this Avar embassy is wrongly assigned in the *Historia Miscella*; wherefore I rather assent to Victor of Tunnuna, who reports it in the 36th year of Justinian. In the same year, he says, the prince Justinian makes the envoys of the Avars [return] whence they had come. Which words, however, should rather be referred to the second Avar embassy, about which Menander [speaks on] page 101 A. Ibid. same. Of the imperial sword-bearers. Menander, speaking of the same Valentinus at the beginning of book viii, [says], "And this one was of the imperial sword-bearers." They are called *Spatharii* by the Latins, whom Corippus describes thus in book I: "Meanwhile the armor-bearer, scanning the footsteps of his master, Narses stands out tall above all with his head." Ibid. C. To the Utigurs, then to the Zali, page 109. B. The Utigurs are said to be neighbors to the Turks. Ibid. same. That since the rulers of the. Scribe at my risk, "the rulers of the Antes," and a little later, where it is read, "being pressed by the incursions of the [text corrupted]," read, "incursions of the Antes." And not long after I amend, "he is clothed with power among the Antes." Then in the same chapter scribe, "they were cutting the land of the Antes," which indeed I wonder was not noticed by the interpreter. Concerning the Antes, see Procopius and Jordanes. Page 101 D. Read, "having been led." What follows thereafter is from book ii of the histories of Menander, concerning the envoys of the Avars, who came to Byzantium during the reign of Justin, in order to receive the customary gifts which had been given to them by Justinian, from Justin as well. Namely, sashes variegated with gold, which Menander calls *seiras* or *kalodia*. But the Avars used to bind their hair with these sashes or bands, as the *Historia Miscella* book xvi teaches, whence Corippus in book I says: "The race of the Avars, bound with snake-like hair." Furthermore, this embassy of the Avars was introduced on the 7th day after the inauguration of Justin the Younger, as testified by Corippus in book iii. "The seventh light was present." And soon, "The master announces to the sacred doors of the divine hall that the envoys of the Avars have been ordered to enter. He commands them to be summoned, whom he sees with voice and mind benign, to be following the sacred footsteps of the clement lord." The rest of Corippus's narrative agrees entirely with Menander's history. Page 105 C. Into the land of the Franks. The Avars, after the death of Clothar, broke into Thuringia and waged war with Sigisbert, as Paul writes in the *History of the Lombards* book ii, chapter 40, and Gregory of Tours book IV, which is to be noted as having happened in the first year of Justin the Younger. Therefore Hermannus Contractus wrongly refers it to the year 561, five years before the death of Justinian. Ibid. same. Having admired the apology.
