For the expression is customary both among the Greeks and among the Latins: which figure is called "according to the meaning." Page 105 C. He said it was not holy after so many. It should be written, "he said," etc., as Cantoclarus seems to have read. Page 106 B. If the nations of the Saracens and these Nomads. Read, "If to this nation of Saracens and Nomads," that is, to the nation of the Saracens and Nomads. The word "this" has no small emphasis. "Mixing of heretical poisons," read, "deadly." Page 108 C. Or according to the village, scribe, "mounds." A little later I read, "teach again, how great the multitude of the Avars is, [which] has revolted from the dominion of the Turks." Than which amendment nothing is more certain. Ibid. D. [text corrupted]. After this embassy of the Turks, which entered Constantinople at the beginning of the fourth year of the Emperor Justin, as Menander testifies on page 106 C, there should immediately follow the embassy of Justin to the Turks, which you have on page 151 D. Of which embassy Zemarchus was the leader. Concerning whose embassy to the Turks, see Theophanes of Byzantium in the *Bibliotheca* of Photius, chapter 64. Page 154 D. Called Saces of old. Theophanes of Byzantium [says] the Turks were called Massagetae by the ancients; but by the Persians they are called Kermichiones. Ibid. same. In the second year of the fifteen-year cycle. It should have been translated, "In the second Indiction." For these words of Menander signify this: although the Greeks more frequently call it *epinemesis*, which the Latins call Indiction. Moreover, this passage is greatly to be noted, which contains notable characters of the times. For Menander says that Zemarchus, to whom the Turkish embassy had been entrusted by the emperor, had set out from Byzantium at the beginning of the month of August, with the fourth year of the Emperor Justin already affected, in the second indiction: but for [text corrupted], it should be read, [text corrupted]. For Justin had entered the empire in the fifteenth indiction, on the fifteenth day of the month of November. And so then when Zemarchus set out, the third indiction had almost reached its end, and on the next Kalends of September, in- 31
ACROASES. — NOTICE BY NIC. MARLE FOGGINI. 990
[He was] Domestic of the Schools and of the Eastern scholai, who, having routed and destroyed the Barbarians, restored [it] to the Roman Empire, under the Emperor Romanus the Younger, in the year of Christ 961. The following five Acroases celebrate and embellish this expedition, which was happily completed with the greatest speed by Nicephorus at the command of the Roman Emperor. Maltretus once intended to publish them, but they were first edited recently by the noble Venetian Flaminius Cornelius in his commentary on Sacred Crete, Vol. I, p. 269. No other source containing them is known until the discovery of an old codex, besides the Vatican manuscript marked number 997, with letters now almost fading, and written by a notary rather than a scribe, from which both Maltretus, through the care of Allatius, and Cornelius received them. Therefore, having been ordered to review and reprint them, I could only achieve this: to consult the Vatican codex itself again and compare it with the edition of Cornelius, a labor which I completed not without fruit, although I remain hesitant as to whether I myself, both because of the defects of the codex and the singular style of a poet of a later age, have fully grasped everything. Wherefore I shall indicate my certain doubts and conjectures in the subjoined annotations, where I shall also pursue further matters that might explain the author's meaning and the history of events more clearly.
Who Theodosius the Deacon, who composed these Acroases, was, is entirely obscure: but it is by no means permissible to doubt that he was a deacon of the Church of Constantinople. Furthermore, from the epistle in which he dedicates his Acroases to Nicephorus, it is clearly evident that he brought them to light after the premature death of the Emperor Romanus, although they were composed while he was still alive. For thus does Theodosius address Nicephorus: "As in the person, therefore, the beginning of the iambics seems to be directed toward our former emperor, but the main point of the praises toward whom [it is directed], you will know immediately." The beginning of the iambics, therefore, at first glance seems to pertain to our recently deceased emperor: but to whom the sum of the praises especially refers, you will immediately understand.
Moreover, from the same epistle it is openly understood that he offered them to Nicephorus after he had added to the victory over Crete a victory also over the Eastern Agarenes, having overthrown their metropolis, Chalepus: "Accept, therefore," he says, "even if they are lacking in worth, the stammerings of Theodosius, and permit [me] to write about the destruction and capture of Chalepus." At these words, one is struck with wonder at how Cornelius could have noted that he was completely ignorant as to why Theodosius, having begun a discourse about Crete, should have introduced a mention of Chalepus. For no historian, ancient or modern, overlooks the fact that Nicephorus Phocas first subdued the Cretan Saracens, and then indeed the Chalepian ones. Hear Cedrenus, *Hist. Comp.*, p. 645: "Nicephorus Phocas, as was said before, having been ordered to return from Crete, was not permitted to enter the imperial city, but was ordered to arrive in the East with the whole army; for Chabdanus, recovering himself from his previous defeat, began to rise again, and having gathered an army fit for battle, was expected to attack the Roman leaders and affairs; but Phocas, having arrived in Syria and having routed him in a pitched battle, and having defeated him by force, and having driven him into the interior of Syria, sacked the city of Beroea, except for the acropolis."
Nicephorus Phocas, as we have said, was ordered to depart from Crete in such a way that he was not allowed to enter Constantinople; but it was commanded that he should cross into the East with the whole army. For Chabdanus, recovering from the defeat he had received, was already rising up; and having gathered an army suitable for battle, he seemed already about to move against the Romans. Phocas, when he came into Syria, overcame and routed him in battle, and drove him into inner Syria; he devastated the city of Beroea, except for the citadel. The same is also narrated by Michael Glycas in his *Annals*, Part IV, p. 304, and both bring forward this cause for the so anxious expedition of Nicephorus into the East: that Joseph Bringas, who held power in the court, had cunningly placed before Romanus the rumor that the victor of Crete was to be raised to the Empire.
But indeed, Cedrenus and Glycas seem to differ in this from Leo the Deacon, a contemporary writer: that they write that Nicephorus, returning from Crete, was not permitted to come to Constantinople, but was ordered to go straight to Syria, whereas he, on the contrary, testifies that Nicephorus, returning from Crete, not only came to Constantinople, but even triumphed in Constantinople, and from there later set out for Syria. That Leo narrated the matter thus is known from the Latin excerpts of the history of his own times, which he extended up to the death of John Tzimiskes, reported by Pagi in his *Critica in Annal. Baron.*, Vol. III, p. 875, and Vol. IV, p. 5; for the entire history of Leo has not yet been published in Greek, although Combefisius left it prepared for the press.
However, if I am not mistaken, Symeon the Master and Logothete in his *Annals* at the end opens an easy way to reconcile Cedrenus and Glycas with Leo the Deacon on this point as well, who speaks thus regarding the triumph of Nicephorus for the defeat of Crete: "The Emperor, having learned of the capture of Crete, gave glory to God; but not long after, Nicephorus, the Domestic of the Schools, by the command of the Emperor, entered the city, and with the spoils and the captives came on foot from his house to the hippodrome, together with the Emir of Crete, called Kouroupas, and all his relatives, all the Saracens wearing white robes; and the Magister was deemed worthy of great honors and gifts by the Emperor; but the Emir, having received many gifts and gold and silver from the Emperor, was permitted to live in the villages with his children, but was not raised to the senate, because they did not wish to be baptized; and the Emperor Romanus sent Nicephorus the Magister back to the East against the arrogant Chabdanus; and he, having gone, first attacked the castle of Chalepus, from which also a great gathering of spoils was made." The Emperor, when he learned that Crete had been captured, rendered praises and thanks to God.
THEODOSIUS THE DEACON: those who served as generals after them, obtained for themselves great glory from their writings even up to this present time. And the one is sung and celebrated by Homer, while the others are sung and celebrated by Plutarch and others even now. Thus, in the person of [the speaker], the beginning of the iambics seems to pertain to our ancient emperor. But you shall know immediately to whom the sum of the praises is directed. FIRST ACROASIS. Old Rome, do not envy the new master, by opposing your generals to the new [Rome]; and those who were emperors after them obtained for themselves great glory from their writings even up to this present time. And the one is praised and celebrated by Homer, while these are praised and celebrated by Plutarch and others even now. The beginning of the iambics, therefore, seems to pertain in a certain way to our ancient emperor; but you shall know immediately to whom the sum of the praises is directed. FIRST ACROASIS. O old Rome, do not envy the new master, by opposing your generals to the new Rome: for undeservedly, Scipio,
996 with an empty impulse, and in vain, plundered the entire wealth of glory. For there was some strength, or power of Carthage, when, standing before a battle of ten thousand swords, it sent calamities to the one being ravaged. Who was Sulla the general, who cast the burned city of Athens, defeated by that very slaughter? Who was Caesar, who ran through the armies in the daily confused clashes? Who was that man who was defeated in a fleet, and exhausted all the riches of glory with an empty effort; what strength was present, not the whole power of Carthage, when, entering battle with countless drawn swords, he brought destructive ruin upon the conquered city. Who was that general Sulla, who subjected the city of Athens, seized by rapid flames, overcome by this very destruction? Who was Caesar, who ran through the daily inescapable conflicts of armies? Cornelius translates the words "our ancient emperor" less correctly: "veterem nostrum imperatorem," when he should have translated it as "deceased." says Florus lib. II, cap. 6, p. 292, "the fates had decreed so great a name from Africa." He, as a very young man, having set out for Africa, declared war against the Carthaginians, in which he conducted himself so brilliantly that he conquered Carthage in a short time. "Made Consul before the years," says Sex. Aur. Victor de Viris illust. cap. 49, "with the colleague conceding, he crossed into Africa with the fleet." He broke through the camps of Hasdrubal and Syphax in one night. He overcame Hannibal, who had been recalled from Italy. He imposed laws on the conquered Carthaginians. Theodosius adds that the whole strength of Carthage was not present at that time, because Hannibal was staying in Italy with a sufficiently large army, intending to attack Rome. "not great in deed, but most brilliant in glory," calls Plutarch in the life of Sulla p. 102. For the Athenians at that time, as Velleius observes, Histor. Rom. lib. II, cap. 25, oppressed by the arms of Mithridates, men of the most miserable condition, while they were held by enemies, were besieged by friends; and they had their spirits outside the walls, their bodies, serving necessity, inside the walls. Furthermore, at the time when Sulla was besieging Athens, they suffered great damage from fire, first ignited by the Athenians themselves, who were eager to burn the hostile machines: which then burned even more fiercely by Sulla's design, while after the capture of Piraeus "he burned most things, among which was the armory of Philo, an admired work," as Plutarch testifies, in the given place, page 65. Theodosius looks to this, while, eager to be angry at Sulla's virtue, he affirms that he accomplished nothing brilliant in the siege of Athens. Which, however, you will easily restore if you supply with us, for the sake of the codex, the article "the" before the word "slaughter." Licinius Lucullus and M. Aurelius Cotta, the Cretan war was unsuccessfully begun by M. Antonius and completed by Q. Caecilius Metellus. But when Metellus, elated by victory, acted too cruelly toward the captives, many surrendered themselves to Pompey, who was staying in Asia. Regarding this matter, Florus, Epitome of Roman History, book I, cap. 7, says: Metellus then, having ravaged the whole island with fire and sword, reduced it within fortresses and cities, and Gortyn, and Erythraea, and, as the Greeks are accustomed to say, the mother of cities, Cydonia: and he acted so cruelly toward the captives that most killed themselves with poison, and others sent their surrender to the absent Pompey. Pompey, however, having returned to Rome, not only triumphed over Mithridates and Tigranes, who had been subdued in Asia, but also celebrated another triumph over the Cretan war: having brought with him the leaders from Crete who had defected to him, diminishing Metellus's triumph, although in reality he had taken no part in the Cretan war. Theodosius, therefore, in these verses compares Pompey with the Roman, or rather with Nicephorus, so that he may the more extol the virtue of the same Nicephorus, since the glory of the Cretan victory was truly owed to him, while on the contrary Pompey had usurped the praise of that old Cretan war, and, as if he had fought in person, presumed to display another triumph over the same Crete at Rome. interlinear, written by the hand of the same writer, as it seems, the Great Caesar, which Cornelius also noticed, but he passes over entirely the conjecture of what it might mean. Therefore, I want you to know that it refers to verse 13, which in the codex is distinguished into three columns composed in order from the three verses following it, the upper one stands as verse 16. For since in v. 13 Theodosius, with the name suppressed, made mention of a certain emperor more obscurely, the writer of the codex added The Great Caesar, designating Augustus, of whom we have already clearly shown above that Theodosius was in fact speaking. [Above the word Pompey, in the codex is written: ... The Great Caesar. Weigh this. CORNEL. A notable form of the name compared to that which others constantly use; especially since it does not appear for what reason Theodosius changed the name, which does not conflict with the iambic verse. Even more memorable is what is read below III, 209: Brutus, who stained the battle of Pompey With blood. For who would believe that Theodosius was so ignorant of history as to believe that Brutus stained his hands with the blood of Pompey? Wherefore Cornelius suspects that there should be a correction to Caesar; which correction Fogginius rightly judged to be devoid of probability, himself thinking the passage to be mutilated. Which is no more probable. But in the former place, of which we began to speak, there are also other things that delay the reader. For first, it is not so certain, as Fogginius thinks, that the actions taken by Pompey to diminish the praise of Metellus in the Cretan war are being looked at; for it appears that all the praise of the deeds of Pompey is included; nor does the sense of the word wealth-gathering emerge clear from that interpretation; nor, finally, does it appear what meaning the little words have. If, on the contrary, credit can be given to the glossator who thought of Julius Caesar (for what Fogginius thinks, that by the words The Great Caesar Augustus is meant, can in no way be proven), first the commemoration of the supreme leader is adapted to the poet's plan; then wealth-gathering can be taken regarding the wealth amassed in the Gallic war (Plutarch calls it Gallic wealth in the Life of Caesar, c. 29) or even regarding those which he took from the treasury at the beginning of the civil war; finally verse 17, with our amendment admitted, he may have fashioned everything according to his own pleasure, and written nothing about the true history of Troy. Dio Chrysostom had already objected to this crime against Homer in oration 11, which he composed entirely to revile Homer, where among other things, he says on page 157, These things have been said by me, as I already said, not for the sake of accusation, but because Homer was the most courageous of men toward falsehood and was no less confident and prided himself on telling the truth; for thus to those observing, nothing appears paradoxical or unbelievable of the things shown by me, but small and human falsehoods compared to divine and great ones. Furthermore, these things have been said by me, as I said, not for the sake of accusing, but for indicating that Homer was the most audacious of men in lies: and no less confident and boastful in telling lies than in truth. For thus to those paying attention, those things which I say he lied about will seem very small and of no value. For they are truly human lies, and very believable, if they are compared to those things which he lied about regarding the divine and immense nature.
